Testing for Regression Heteroskedasticity with High-Dimensional Random Forests
Chien-Ming Chi Academia Sinica

Abstract
Statistical inference for high-dimensional regression heteroskedasticity is an important but under-explored problem. The current paper aims at filling this gap by proposing two tests, namely the variance difference test and the variance difference Breusch-Pagan test, for assessing high-dimensional regression heteroskedasticity. The former tests whether an explanatory feature of interest is associated with the conditional variance of a response variable, while the latter tests heteroskedasticity in the regression, which is known to be the Breusch-Pagan test problem. To formally establish the tests, we have derived rigorous P-values and test sizes, and analyzed the test power under a nonparametric heteroskedastic data generating model with high-dimensional input features. Such a model setting takes into account high-dimensional applications with flexible structures of heteroskedasticity and features having interaction effects on the mean of the response; these are common applications in many fields such as biology. Our methods leverage machine learning mean prediction methods such as random forests and use knockoff variables as negative controls. Particularly, the definition of knockoffs for our test statistics is more flexible than the original definition of knockoffs, and we give a detailed comparison of these two definitions and discuss the advantages of our knockoffs. The satisfactory empirical performance of the proposed tests is illustrated with simulation results and an HIV (Human Immunodeficiency Virus) case study.



Keywords:
Hypothesis test; Asymptotic null distribution; Knockoff features.

1
Introduction

Statistical inference for heteroskedasticity has been widely applied to estimation efficiency improvement
(
Tsay 2005
)
, financial asset allocation
(
McNeil
et al. 2015
)
, genetic analysis
(
Wang
et al. 2021
;
Hill and
Mulder 2010
)
, etc. The importance of heteroskedasticity has encouraged the development of various modeling methods
(
Smyth 1989
;
Sesia
et al. 2019
;
Storey
et al. 2020
;
Lee and
Nelder 2006
)
addressing heteroskedastic disturbance. The prediction accuracy of heteroskedasticity modeling methods has continued to be improved thanks to the emerging machine learning ideas such as ensemble trees
(
Pratola et al. 2020
;
Hahn
et al. 2020
)
, but rigorous inferences for regression heteroskedasticity seem to be under-explored
(
Cleasby and
Nakagawa 2011
;
Ayroles
et al. 2015
)
. The goal of the current paper is to fill this gap by formally establishing tests for high-dimensional regression heteroskedasticity.

Let us further clarify our motivation with an example. Suppose we want to improve the Human Immunodeficiency Virus Type 1 (HIV-1) treatment by studying the distributional relations between a specific set of genetic mutations and the HIV-1 drug resistance level for certain HIV-1 drugs
(
Rhee et al. 2006
)
.
Particularly, if we want to apply the Breusch-Pagan test
(
Breusch and
Pagan 1979
)
to draw the inference about whether the set of genetic mutations are associated with the conditional variance of the HIV-1 drug resistance level, we are facing at least two challenges. First, it is likely that mutations have non-linear mean effects on the drug resistance level
(
White 1980
)
. Second, the number of mutations can be of the same size as or larger than the sample size. The Breusch-Pagan test may not perform well or may not be applicable in this application since it considers linear regression models with heteroskedastic disturbances and fixed feature dimensionality. Despite recent advances in making inferences for heteroskedasticity, some major limitations remain. For example, the related work reviewed below does not consider high-dimensional features with non-linear mean effects on the response variable. The proposed tests in this paper are meant to improve upon these limitations, and provide more detailed inferences about heteroskedasticity based on each feature. To appreciate our contributions, we now formally introduce the test problems as follows; the details of our HIV-1 case study are deferred to Section
6
.

Let a random variable
Y
and a
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{X}
denote the response variable of interest and the explanatory feature vector, respectively. To understand how these explanatory features are associated with regression heteroskedasticity, we assume the statistical heteroskedastic model

Y=m(\bm{X})+\varepsilon\times\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})

(1)

for some
S^{*}\subset\{1,\dots,p\}
,
where
\varepsilon
is a model error independent of all else variables such that
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon)=0
and Var
(\varepsilon)=1
,
m:\mathbb{R}^{p}\longmapsto\mathbb{R}
is the mean function, and that
\bm{X}_{S^{*}}=(X_{j},j\in S^{*})^{\top}
and
\zeta:\mathbb{R}^{|S^{*}|}\longmapsto(0,\infty)
are respectively a small subset of relevant features and the standard deviation function such that
\textnormal{Var}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\big]>0
if and only if
S^{*}\not=\emptyset
. In model (
1
), the subset of active features of the mean function is not indicated since it is of minor interest in this paper. Examples of sparse non-linear
m(\cdot)
and monotonic
\zeta(\cdot)
are respectively in Section
3.1
and Section
4.1
. With model (
1
), we are interested in testing

H_{0}:j\not\in S^{*}

(2)

for the
j
th feature of interest. In this paper,
X_{j}
is a null feature if and only if
j\not\in S^{*}
. With
\zeta(\bm{X}_{\emptyset})\coloneqq\zeta_{0}
for some
\zeta_{0}>0
, we are also interested in testing

H_{0}:S^{*}=\emptyset,

(3)

which is known to be the Breusch-Pagan test.

These tests are widely used in literature. For example, recent studies have found genetic effects on the variability of a fruit fly’s behavior decision
(
Ayroles
et al. 2015
)
and a single-nucleotide polymorphism (SNP) that is associated with the variability of body mass index and height
(
Wang
et al. 2021
)
. Their works essentially test (
2
) for each feature in their applications in order to find the significant features. A challenge there is that the selected relevant features are not associated with the underlying response variable through the mean function, which means that the existing methods such as the Lasso
(
Tian
et al. 2016
)
and feature importance MDI
(
Breiman 2001
)
cannot be directly used for making inferences about (
2
) or (
3
). Hence, the aforementioned papers and most existing literature for genetic analysis and biological works
(
Wang
et al. 2021
;
Ayroles
et al. 2015
;
Brown
et al. 2014
;
Hill and
Mulder 2010
)
have developed statistical inference procedures for their own applications. However, these inference procedures are more or less heuristic and have no theoretical foundations. To our knowledge, the test problems (
2
) and (
3
) have not yet been formally considered for applications with high-dimensional input features that may have complicated effects such as interaction effects on the response variable. Hence, the proposed tests,
namely, the variance difference (VD) and variance difference Breusch-Pagan (VDBP) tests, contribute as the first rigorous tests for the respective null hypothesis test problems (
2
) and (
3
) in high-dimensional applications with flexible mean and standard deviation functions in model (
1
).

Three advantages of the proposed tests are introduced as follows. First, since we are interested in the inference for conditional variance in model (
1
), the first step of the VD test is to center the observed response variables by subtracting the estimated conditional means from the responses. We employ random forests
(
Breiman 2001
)
as the centering method for the VD test. As a result, the VD test allows non-linear effects of the explanatory features on the conditional mean of the response. Particularly, our simulation experiments show that the VD test is applicable when mean function
m(\bm{X})
in model (
1
) consists of interaction components, which are common model components in genetic analysis
(
Phillips 2008
;
Brown
et al. 2014
)
. Second, the VD test considers model (
1
) with a wide class of monotonic standard deviation functions. Hence, our tests are robust in practice and can reduce the chance of false positive errors due to model misspecification. Third, our tests allow high-dimensional input features. These practical advantages are also enjoyed by the VDBP test for the Breusch-Pagan test problem (
3
).

On the theory side, we establish the asymptotic null distributions of the VD and VDBP tests, which enables us to calculate their test sizes and the much needed P-values. We show that the consistency of mean estimation at the centering step is crucial in order to establish the asymptotic null distributions. In addition, assuming a general model setting satisfying (
1
), we show that the consistency of the proposed tests (i.e., test has asymptotic power one) under their respective alternative hypotheses depends on the sample size, the input feature dimensionality, the accuracy of the conditional mean estimation at the centering step, and important model parameters such as the variation level of the standard deviation function. In light of the importance of high-dimensional consistency of random forests to our tests, some results of random forests consistency are reviewed in Section
3.1
. In fact, existing theoretical results of high-dimensional consistency under model (
1
) are mostly for random forests, which is a major reason that we consider random forests instead of other machine learning prediction methods
(
Chen and
Guestrin 2016
;
Chollet
et al. 2015
;
Friedberg et al. 2020
)
as the centering method in this paper.

Related work aiming at making inferences for general models usually assumes fixed feature dimensionality.
To relax the linear mean function assumption made by the Breusch-Pagan test, Su & Ullah
(
Storey
et al. 2020
)
used the local polynomial regression as the centering method for testing (
3
), while Dumitrascu et al.
(
Dumitrascu et al. 2019
)
proposed a Bayesian test for the test problem (
3
). Meanwhile, DGLM
(
Smyth 1989
)
and DHGLM
(
Lee and
Nelder 2006
)
rely on the generalized linear models for outputing heuristic P-values of the estimated heteroskedasticity coefficients for testing (
2
); their P-values lack theoretical foundations. Despite a broad recognition of the importance of test problems (
2
)–(
3
), rigorous tests for applications with high-dimensional features are quite under-explored; particularly, the aforementioned methods
(
Storey
et al. 2020
;
Dumitrascu et al. 2019
;
Smyth 1989
;
Lee and
Nelder 2006
)
as well as most references therein all assume fixed dimentionality. Li & Yao
(
Li and Yao 2019
)
derived a rigorous high-dimensional test for (
3
) assuming a linear mean function with the ordinary least-square method as the centering method. In addition, there are a few high-dimensional consistent model selection methods for selecting active features in the standard deviation function
(
Daye
et al. 2012
;
Chiou
et al. 2020
;
Doss and
McFowland III 2022
)
. However, they either assumed specific structures of mean and standard deviation functions, which may restrict their applicability, or lack theoretical foundations. Moreoever, these methods cannot be applied to test problems (
2
)–(
3
) directly.

It is worth mentioning that the advantages of the proposed tests are partly attributed to the use of model-X knockoff (hereafter, knockoff for short) features
(
Candès
et al. 2018
)
as negative controls in establishing the VD and VDBP tests. The knockoffs for our tests are defined in a coordinate-wise fashion in Section
2.1
. For example, to calculate the VD test statistic for testing (
2
), we need the knockoff feature of the
j
th explanatory feature. The population
j
th knockoff feature, denoted by
\widetilde{X}_{j}
, is such that
(\varepsilon\times\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}}),X_{j})
and
(\varepsilon\times\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}}),\widetilde{X}_{j})
have the same distribution under (
2
). We then use the
j
th knockoff as the negative control for establishing our tests. The definition of coordinate-wise knockoffs is more flexible than the original one in
(
Candès
et al. 2018
)
; a detailed comparison between these two definitions is in Section
2.1
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. The VD and VDBP tests are introduced in Section
2
, with the analysis of their test sizes and P-values in Section
3.2
and selection power in Section
4.2
. The simulation study and HIV-1 data study are in Sections
5
–
6
, respectively. Technical proofs are all deferred to the Supplementary Material.

1.1
Notation

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
denote the probability space, and
\mathcal{R}
be the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}
. For any vector
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, let
\vec{x}_{S}
denote the subvector with entries in the index subset
S
, and let
\vec{x}_{-j}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j+1},\dots,x_{p})^{\top}
; such expressions are also used for random vectors. This paper uses the following notation. 1)

|S|
denotes the number of elements in a set
S
. 2)
\lfloor x\rceil
denotes the closest integer of
x\in\mathbb{R}
. 3) The indicator function is denoted by
\bm{1}_{\{\cdot\}}
. 4) The symbol
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}
means convergence in distribution. In addition, we define
\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})=\zeta_{0}
when
S^{*}=\emptyset
for some
\zeta_{0}>0
, where
\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})
is given in model (
1
). Moreover, we define
\zeta(\vec{z})=\zeta_{0}
for
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{0}
when
S^{*}=\emptyset
; particularly, we have
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{0}}\zeta(\vec{z})=\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{0}}\zeta(\vec{z})=\zeta_{0}
when
S^{*}=\emptyset
.

2
Hypothesis testing procedures

The VD and VDBP tests are presented in this section, with coordinate-wise knockoff features, the key ingredients for establishing our tests, introduced in Section
2.1
. The VD test statistic for testing (
2
) involves a break
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
on the
j
th coordinate, while the VDBP test statistic for (
3
) involves
p
breaks
a_{1},\dots,a_{p}
on each coordinate. To see the intuition for the use of breaks and coordinate-wise knockoffs, consider an example at population level where feature vector
\bm{X}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{p})^{\top}
and its coordinate-wise knockoff vector
\widetilde{\bm{X}}=(\widetilde{X}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{X}_{p})^{\top}
(formally defined in Section
2.1
) are uniformly distributed on
[0,1]^{2p}
,
Y=\varepsilon\times\sqrt{\exp{(X_{1})}}
with an independent standard Gaussian model error
\varepsilon
, and constant break
a_{j}=\frac{1}{2}
for each
j
. Then,
X_{j}
is relevant if and only if
\big|\textnormal{Var}(Y|X_{j}>a_{j})-\textnormal{Var}(Y|\widetilde{X}_{j}>a_{j})\big|>0
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
. This idea for identifying relevant features at population level is applicable to cases where
\bm{X}
may have a general distribution, and is our basic idea for establishing the VD and VDBP tests when samples are involved.

Now, the VD and VDBP tests with given breaks are introduced in Sections
2.2
–
2.3
, respectively. These tests are suitable for our real data study in Section
6
, where
a_{j}=0.5
since
X_{j}
’s are binary for each
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
. Meanwhile, for applications without given breaks, we want to select a break
\widehat{a}_{j}
on each coordinate in a data-driven fashion so as to make our tests as powerful as possible. We introduce in Section
2.4
the VD and VDBP tests with data-driven break selection. Our break selection aims at finding potential breaks for test consistency (i.e., the asymptotic test power is one), which are not necessarily the best break in terms of test power on each coordinate. Let
\Phi(\cdot)
denote the cumulative distribution function for the standard normal distribution and
\Phi^{-1}(\cdot)
be the inverse function of
\Phi(\cdot)
.

2.1
Coordinate-wise model-X knockoff features

2.1.1
Definition and comparison with the original model-X knockoffs

The model-X coordinate-wise knockoffs are defined in Definition
1
below. For simplicity, we refer to the original model-X knockoffs and coordinate-wise model-X knockoffs as knockoffs and coordinate-wise knockoffs, respectively.

Definition 1

(Coordinate-wise knockoffs)
.

\widetilde{X}_{j}
is a coordinate-wise knockoff of
X_{j}
if and only if
(\bm{X}_{-j},X_{j})
and
(\bm{X}_{-j},\widetilde{X}_{j})
have the same distribution and
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is independent of
Y
conditional on
\bm{X}
.

It is readily seen from Definition
1
and Definition
2
below that if
\widetilde{\bm{X}}
is a knockoff vector of
\bm{X}
satisfying Definition
2
, then each
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is a coordinate-wise knockoff of
X_{j}
that satisfies Definition
1
for
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, but not the other way around. This implies that a knockoff vector produced by Algorithm 1 of
(
Candès
et al. 2018
)
, which samples
\widetilde{X}_{j}
from the conditional distribution of
X_{j}
on
(\bm{X}_{-j},\widetilde{X}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{X}_{j-1})
from
j=1
to
j=p
, is also a coordinate-wise knockoff vector satisfying Definition
1
. In addition, the approximate knockoffs produced by the existing knockoff generators such as
(
Barber and
Candès 2019
;
Serfling 2009
;
Romano
et al. 2020
;
Jordon
et al. 2018
;
Lu
et al. 2018
)
are also approximate coordinate-wise knockoffs. Moreover, since the assumptions on knockoffs in Definition
1
are more flexible than those in Definition
2
, it is possible to simplify the production procedure of coordinate-wise knockoffs. For example, to generate a vector of coordinate-wise knockoffs
\widetilde{\bm{X}}
satisfying Definition
1
, it suffices to

\textnormal{ Sample }\widetilde{X}_{j}\textnormal{ from the conditional distribution of }X_{j}\textnormal{ on }\bm{X}_{-j}\textnormal{ for each }j\in\{1,\dots,p\},

(4)

which is simpler than Algorithm 1 of
(
Candès
et al. 2018
)
.

Definition 2

(Original knockoffs in
(
Candès
et al. 2018
)
)
.

\widetilde{\bm{X}}
are knockoffs of
\bm{X}
if and only if
1)

(\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}})
and
(\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}})_{\textnormal{swap}(S)}
have the same distribution for each
S\subset\{1,\dots,p\}
, where
(\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}})_{\textnormal{swap}(S)}
are obtained from
(\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}})
by swapping the entries
X_{j}
and
\widetilde{X}_{j}
for each
j\in S
, and
2)

\widetilde{\bm{X}}
is independent of
Y
conditional on
\bm{X}
.

2.1.2
Production of approximate coordinate-wise model-X knockoffs

In light of Definition
1
and (
4
), we may assume a known distribution of
\bm{X}
for coordinate-wise knockoff production. We consider two distributions of
\bm{X}
for our inference applications in this paper: the multivariate Gaussian distribution
(
Candès
et al. 2018
)
and the hidden Markov model (HMM)
(
Serfling 2009
)
. The former distribution for knockoffs was proposed by the original knockoffs inference papers
(
Barber and
Candès 2019
;
Candès
et al. 2018
)
, which is widely used for statistical inference
(
Barber
et al. 2015
)
because of its convenience and stable empirical performance. The latter one is a natural distributional assumption for modeling SNPs in genetic analysis, as have been introduced in
(
Serfling 2009
)
. Since the approximate knockoffs for Definition
2
are also the approximate coordinate-wise knockoffs, as have discussed in Section
2.1.1
, we may generate the approximate coordinate-wise Gaussian and HMM knockoffs respectively by the R packages
knockoff

(
Barber and
Candès 2019
)
and
SNPknock

(
Serfling 2009
)
; these softwares take a sample of feature vectors as input and output the approximate knockoffs.

Besides the existing methods, we have implemented a knockoff generator for producing approximate coordinate-wise Gaussian knockoffs in a coordinate-wise fashion based on the ideas of (
4
) and those introduced in
(
Barber and
Candès 2019
)
. To demonstrate the advantage of our coordinate-wise knockoffs, we have performed a numerical experiment to show that the correlation between a Gaussian variable and its coordinate-wise knockoff tend to be smaller, sometimes much smaller, than the correlation between the Gaussian variable and its knockoff generated by the R package
knockoff

(
Barber and
Candès 2019
)
. To save space, details of our coordinate-wise Gaussian knockoff generator and the results of our numerical experiments are postponed to the Supplementary Material.

2.2
Variance difference test

In this section, we introduce the VD test for hypothesis (
2
) with some
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
of interest and a given break
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
; the subscript
j
of
a_{j}
is dropped when no confusion is possible. Let an inference sample
\{\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
with ideal coordinate-wise knockoff features satisfying Definition
1
or approximate ones be given such that
\{\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
(\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}},Y)
are i.i.d. random vectors. In addition, we are given a training sample for constructing

\widehat{m}(\bm{X})
, the random forests
(
Breiman 2001
)
estimate of mean function
m(\bm{X})
in model (
1
). We consider two set-ups for the training sample. First, the regression trees model is trained on the inference sample; in other words, the training and inference samples are the same. In our simulation experiments and data study, we find that the VD test is easy-to-use in practice under this setting and has satisfactory empirical performance. Second, the training sample is assumed to be an independent copy of the inference sample. The analysis of the proposed tests in Sections
3
–
4
assumes the second setting with an independent training sample to simplify technical details. Now, we introduce the VD test in (
6
) below, which is the same testing procedure regardless of whether the training sample is an independent copy of the inference sample or not.

For each
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and some given break
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

\begin{split}T_{l}(a)&=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{1}_{X_{il}\in(-\infty,a]}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{il}\in(-\infty,a]})(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2},\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{l}^{2}(a)&=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big[(\bm{1}_{X_{il}\in(-\infty,a]}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{il}\in(-\infty,a]})(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}-\widehat{\mu}_{l}(a)\Big]^{2},\end{split}

(5)

where
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})
and
\widehat{\mu}_{l}(a)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{1}_{X_{il}\in(-\infty,a]}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{il}\in(-\infty,a]})(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}
. The VD test statistic with break
a\in\mathbb{R}
for the
j
th feature and a test threshold
t>0
is given as follows.

\begin{split}\textnormal{Test statistic }&:\ T_{j}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1},\\
\textnormal{Test }&:\textnormal{ We reject the null hypothesis in \eqref{null.1} if }\left|T_{j}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1}\right|>t.\end{split}

(6)

We show in Section
3.2
that when the rejection threshold
t=-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the asymptotic size is at most
\alpha
. Meanwhile, we take the frequentist interpretation of P-value, which is the probability of obtaining a test statistic at least as extreme as the observed test statistic, while assuming the null hypothesis and model regularity assumptions. In Section
3.2
, we show that the P-value of test (
6
) is estimated by
2\Phi\big\{-|T_{j}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1}|\big\}
. It is noteworthy that if we establish tests for all
1\leq j\leq p
, then multiplicity correction is needed in practice.

To have some intuition for how
T_{j}(a)
works, consider its population version as follows. For
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, define

\begin{split}T_{l}^{(\star)}(a)&\coloneqq n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{1}_{X_{il}\in(-\infty,a]}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{il}\in(-\infty,a]})\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{2},\\
\mu_{l}(a)&\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\big\{(\bm{1}_{X_{l}\in(-\infty,a]}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{l}\in(-\infty,a]})\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{2}\big\}.\end{split}

(7)

When the residual
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}
is an
accurate estimate of
\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}
, which is the case when
\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})
is an accurate estimate of
m(\bm{X}_{i})
, it is seen from (
7
) that the intuitions for test statistic (
8
) below with
\mu_{j}(a)=0
and the test statistic in (
6
) above are the same. Given the form of
T_{j}^{(\star)}(a)
and the i.i.d. observations, it is possible to show that under some regularity conditions,

\sqrt{n}\big\{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}T_{j}^{(\star)}(a)-\mu_{j}(a)\big\}\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1}

(8)

is asymptotically normally distributed with zero mean and unit variance. To make use of statistic (
8
) for testing (
2
), we need to know the value of
\mu_{j}(a)
under the null hypothesis. To this end, we rely on Lemma
1
below. Lemma
1
is a basic result of exchangeability
(
Candès
et al. 2018
)
for null features and their knockoffs.

Lemma 1
.

For a null feature
X_{j}
with
j\not\in S^{*}
and its coordinate-wise knockoff
\widetilde{X}_{j}
,
(\varepsilon\times\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}}),X_{j})
and
(\varepsilon\times\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}}),\widetilde{X}_{j})
have the same distribution.

By Lemma
1
, for each
j\not\in S^{*}
and each
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}\big\{\bm{1}_{X_{j}\in(-\infty,a]}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{2}\big\}=\mathbb{E}\big\{\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{j}\in(-\infty,a]}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{2}\big\},

(9)

which implies
\mu_{j}(a)=0
, and the desired test inference under null hypothesis (
2
) can be established for population test statistic (
8
) accordingly. The result of (
9
) is therefore the key for understanding the intuition for test statistic
T_{j}(a)
. Similar ideas have been used for testing conditional independence; for example, see
(
König et al. 2021
)
.

2.3
Variance difference Breusch-Pagan Test

In this section, we introduce the VDBP test for (
3
) with given breaks, and assume
a_{1}=\dots=a_{p}=a
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
for simplicity. As in Section
2.2
, we are given an inference sample
\mathcal{X}_{n}
of size
n
and a training sample
\mathcal{X}_{0}
for constructing the regression trees estimate of
m(\bm{X})
, which is denoted by
\widehat{m}(\bm{X})
. The inference sample
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is split into
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
with
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and
n_{1}=\lfloor\frac{1}{3}n\rceil
. Hence, we have three samples:
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\{\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
for constructing the test statistics,
\mathcal{X}_{2}=\{\bm{U}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i},V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
for screening out features (see details below), and a training sample
\mathcal{X}_{0}
for constructing
\widehat{m}(\bm{X})
. For the training sample, the two set-ups are the same as have mentioned in Section
2.2
. Now, let us introduce the VDBP testing procedure.

The VDBP test has two steps. First, we select the most active feature based on sample
\mathcal{X}_{2}
. For each
1\leq l\leq p
, let
G_{l}(a)=n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{2}}(\bm{1}_{U_{il}\in(-\infty,a]}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{U}_{il}\in(-\infty,a]})(\widehat{\eta}_{i})^{2}
and
\widehat{l}=\arg\max_{1\leq l\leq p}|G_{l}(a)|
, where
\widehat{\eta}_{i}=V_{i}-\widehat{m}(\bm{U}_{i})
. Next, we perform a VD test for the selected feature
\widehat{l}
in (
10
) below. Let
T_{l}(a)
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{l}^{2}(a)
depending on
\mathcal{X}_{1}
for each
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
be given as in (
5
) in Section
2.2
; notice that
\mathcal{X}_{1}
instead of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is used at this step. The VDBP test with a given break
a\in\mathbb{R}
and a test threshold
t>0
is given as follows.

\begin{split}\textnormal{Test statistic }&:\ T_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]^{-1},\\
\textnormal{Test }&:\textnormal{ We reject the null hypothesis in \eqref{BP.1} if }\left|T_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]^{-1}\right|>t.\end{split}

(10)

We show in Section
3.2
that with the rejection threshold
t=-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the asymptotic size is at most
\alpha
; meanwhile, the frequentist P-value is estimated by
2\Phi\big\{-|T_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]^{-1}|\big\}
.

2.4
VD and VDBP tests with break selection

In this section, we introduce the VD and VDBP tests with break selection and the intuition for selecting good breaks from break candidates. Let
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\{Y_{i},\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
,
\mathcal{X}_{2}=\{V_{i},\bm{U}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
, and a training sample
\mathcal{X}_{0}
be as given in Section
2.3
, with
\widehat{m}(\bm{X})
denoting the regression trees estimate of
m(\bm{X})
trained on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
denote the inference sample at hand, which is split into
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
with
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and
n_{1}=\lfloor\gamma_{0}n\rceil
for some constant
0<\gamma_{0}<1
. We set
\gamma_{0}
to
\frac{2}{3}
and
\frac{1}{3}
for the VD and VDBP tests, respectively.

Let
G_{l}(\kappa_{1,l}),\dots,G_{l}(\kappa_{R,l})
be defined as in Section
2.3
for each
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, where
R>1
and break candidates
\{\kappa_{1,l},\dots,\kappa_{R,l}\}
are some parameters predetermined by users with
\kappa_{r,l}<\kappa_{r+1,l}
. Practically, for each
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, we set
(\kappa_{1,l},\kappa_{R,l})
to be respectively the first and third quartiles of
\{U_{il}\}_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
, and that
\kappa_{r,l}
’s are evenly distributed on
[\kappa_{1,l},\kappa_{R,l}]
with
R=100
. Here, for simplicity, we take
\kappa_{r,l}
’s as some predetermined constants that do not depend on sample or coordinate index, and we write
\kappa_{r}=\kappa_{r,l}
for each
l
. The break selection is given as follows.

\widehat{a}_{l}=\arg\max_{\kappa\in\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{R}\}}|G_{l}(\kappa)|\ \ \textnormal{ for }\ \ l\in\{1,\dots,p\}\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad\widehat{l}=\arg\max_{1\leq l\leq p}|G_{l}(\widehat{a}_{l})|,

where
\widehat{a}_{l}
is the sample best break over
\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{R}\}
on the
l
th coordinate.

Let us gain some insight into break selection. To achieve test consistency for VD and VDBP tests, the break selection aims at selecting
\widehat{a}_{l}
on the
l
th coordinate such that
|\mu_{l}(\widehat{a}_{l})|
is uniformly bounded away from zero for each
l\in S^{*}
, where
\mu_{l}(x)
for
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
has been defined in (
7
). We will see in Section
4.2
that

|\mu_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})|
has a nontrivial lower bound in a probability sense if
j\in S^{*}
and

\max_{r\in\{1,\dots,R\}}|\mu_{j}(\kappa_{r})|>\underline{\mu}

(11)

for some signal strength
\underline{\mu}>0
whose value may decrease as the sample size increases. We further show in Example
5
in Section
4.2
that (
11
) holds if (i) the standard deviation function
\zeta(\cdot)
is given as in Example
2
with the variation level of
\zeta(\cdot)
properly bounded away from below, (ii) the range between
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{R}
is reasonably wide and
\kappa_{1}<\dots<\kappa_{R}
are evenly distributed with some constant
R>1
, and (iii) some mild model regularity assumptions are satisfied. Such requirements on
\kappa_{r}
’s justify our data-driven choice of
\kappa_{r,l}
’s described above.

Remark 1
.

Although both of our break selection and covariance change point detection
(
Tibshirani 1996
;
Avanesov and
Buzun 2018
;
Aue et al. 2009
)
make inferences about the standard deviation function, the problem of break selection cannot be seen as a change point detection problem. Particularly, our break selection does not assume piecewise linear
\zeta(\cdot)
, which precludes applications of most existing change point detection techniques to break finding here.

Next, we formally give the VD and VDBP tests with break selection. For each
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
T_{l}(x)
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{l}^{2}(x)
be defined as in (
5
) with sample size
n_{1}
in place of
n
. The VD test with break selection for the
j
th feature and a test threshold
t>0
is given by

\begin{split}\textnormal{Test statistic }&:\ T_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big]^{-1},\\
\textnormal{Test }&:\textnormal{ We reject the null hypothesis in \eqref{null.1} if }\left|T_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big]^{-1}\right|>t.\end{split}

(12)

The VDBP test with break selection and a test threshold
t>0
is given by

\begin{split}\textnormal{Test statistic }&:\ T_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]^{-1},\\
\textnormal{Test }&:\textnormal{ We reject the null hypothesis in \eqref{BP.1} if }\left|T_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]^{-1}\right|>t.\end{split}

(13)

We show in Section
3.2
that when the rejection threshold
t=-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, both asymptotic test sizes of (
12
)–(
13
) are at most
\alpha
, while their respective P-values are estimated by
2\Phi\big\{-|T_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big]^{-1}|\big\}
and
2\Phi\big\{-|T_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]^{-1}|\big\}
.

3
Analysis of VD and VDBP tests under the nulls

We analyze four tests (
6
), (
10
), (
12
), and (
13
) under their respective null hypotheses in Theorems
1
–
4
in Section
3.2
. The asymptotic null distributions of the test statistics depend on the mean estimation accuracy, which is discussed in Section
3.1
. With these results, we can obtain the P-values and test sizes for our inference applications.

Throughout this section,
\{Y_{i},\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i},\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
,
\{V_{i},\bm{U}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i},\eta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
,
(Y,\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}},\varepsilon)
are i.i.d. random vectors where
(Y,\bm{X},\varepsilon)
follows (
1
) and
\widetilde{\bm{X}}
is an ideal coordinate-wise knockoff vector of
\bm{X}
as defined in Definition
1
. We assume to be given three independent samples:
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\{Y_{i},\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
for constructing test statistics,
\mathcal{X}_{2}=\{V_{i},\bm{U}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
for selecting breaks, and a training sample
\mathcal{X}_{0}
for training the regression trees estimate of the mean function, which is denoted by
\widehat{m}(\cdot)
. To simplify the technical analysis in this section, we assume
n_{1}=n_{2}=n
and that the size of the training sample is also
n
. In addition, for tests with a given break
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
on each coordinate, we assume
a_{1}=\dots=a_{p}=a
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for tests with break selection, we assume the same set of constant break candidates across each coordinate, and they are denoted by
\kappa_{r}
’s with
\kappa_{1}<\dots<\kappa_{R}
for some integer
R>1
. We use the notation
x\vee y=\max\{x,y\}
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.

3.1
High-dimensional consistency of random forests

The proposed tests rely on an accurate estimate of mean function
m(\bm{X})
in model (
1
). In this paper, we employ random forests
(
Breiman 2001
)
to cope with the estimation of a potentially highly non-linear sparse mean function
m(\bm{X})
such as Example
1
below.

Example 1
.

Let
\bm{X}
have a uniform distribution on
[0,1]^{p}
and some fixed interger
0<s^{*}\leq p
be given. Let
m(\bm{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{s^{*}}(\beta_{j}X_{j}+\sum_{l\geq j}^{s^{*}}\beta_{lj}X_{l}X_{j})
, in which if
\beta_{lj}\not=0
for some
j\leq l\leq s^{*}
, then
a\times b\geq 0
for every
\{a,b\}\subset\{\beta_{j},\beta_{1j},\dots,\beta_{s^{*}j}\}
where
\beta_{j_{2}j_{1}}=\beta_{j_{1}j_{2}}
; the coefficients are otherwise arbitrary.

Besides the ability to model non-linear mean functions, random forests are considered because their consistency as in Condition
1
below under heteroskedastic model (
1
) has been studied. The random forests estimate is denoted by
\widehat{m}(\bm{X})
, and the dependence of consistency rate
B_{1}
on the training sample size
n
is not indicated for simplicity.

Condition 1
.

\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X})-m(\bm{X})\big]^{2}\leq B_{1}
for some small
B_{1}>0
.

The consistency rate in Condition
1
of a random forests variant under heteroskedastic model (
1
) has been studied in Theorem 5 and Corollary 6 in
(
Biau 2012
)
. There, it is shown that
B_{1}\leq C\times n^{\frac{-0.75}{s^{\star}\times(\log_{e}2)+0.75}}
for some constant
C>0
and all large
n
(training sample size) where
s^{\star}
is the number of active features in
m(\bm{X})
, while assuming independent explanatory features, a Lipschitz continuous
m(\bm{X})
that takes Example
1
into account,
\textnormal{Var}(Y|\bm{X})<C_{2}
for some constant
C_{2}>0
almost surely, and a simplified splitting rule of random forests. In addition, the feature dimensionality
p
is allowed to be much larger than
s^{\star}
as long as the
splitting procedure can track and split the strong coordinates of active features. Besides, recent results
(
Chi
et al. 2020
;
Su and
Ullah 2013
;
Klusowski 2021
)
have analyzed the high-dimensional consistency rate of random forests while assuming the original CART splitting rule and homoscedastic models with general mean functions such as Example
1
. It is possible to extend these results to cases with heteroskedastic models.

3.2
P-values and test sizes under null hypotheses

Let some nondecreasing real sequence
\{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
with
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}\geq 1
and some
c>0
be given for the following regularity conditions.

Condition 2
.

|m(\bm{X})|\leq\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}
almost surely and
|\widehat{m}(\bm{X})|\leq\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}
almost surely for each
n
.

Condition 3
.

\mathbb{P}(\{X_{j}\leq\kappa_{1}\}\cap\{\widetilde{X}_{j}\leq\kappa_{1}\})>c
and
\mathbb{P}(\{X_{j}\leq\kappa_{1}\}\cap\{\widetilde{X}_{j}>\kappa_{R}\})>c
.

Condition 4
.

The training sample
\mathcal{X}_{0}
for
\widehat{m}(\cdot)
is independent of
\{V_{i},\bm{U}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i},\eta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
\{Y_{i},\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i},\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, and
(Y,\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}},\varepsilon)
.

Some comments on these conditions are given as follows. The assumption of an independent training sample in Condition
4
is assumed to simplify the technical analysis for our main results. Condition
2
with
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}=\log{(n)}
is a common assumption for analyzing nonparametric and machine learning predictors
(
Kohler and
Langer 2021
)
. Condition
3
controls the dependence of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
on
X_{j}
conditional on
\bm{X}_{-j}
. Condition
3
with a proper choice of
(c,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{R})
is satisfied if, for example,
(X_{j},\widetilde{X}_{j})
has a bivariate normal distribution with a non-singular covariance matrix.

Theorem
1
below analyzes the asymptotic properties for the VD test (
6
). Based on the results of Theorem
1
, we can obtain the much needed P-value and test size. Recall that in Section
1.1
, we have defined
\zeta(\vec{z})=\zeta_{0}
for
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{|S^{*}|}
when
S^{*}=\emptyset
with constant
\zeta_{0}>0
. Let
s^{*}=|S^{*}|
.

Theorem 1
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{8})<\infty
, Conditions
1
–
2
and Condition
4
. For
all large
n
, each
0<B_{1}<1
, each
t>0
, and any
1\leq j\leq p
such that Condition
3
holds with
(R,\kappa_{1})=(1,a)
and that
j\not\in S^{*}
, it holds that

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|T_{j}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1}\right|\geq t\right)\leq 2\Phi(-t)+D_{n},\end{split}

(14)

where
D_{n}=\varsigma\big\{c\big[\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})\big]^{4}\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{4})\big\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+n^{\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}+(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n})^{2}(\log{n})(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+B_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}})
, in which
\varsigma=t(\log{n})(10n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+4B_{1}^{\frac{1}{8}})+n^{\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
c
is given in Condition
3
.

If
D_{n}=o(1)
, Theorem
1
implies that the test size of the VD test (
6
) is at most
\alpha
plus the negligible term
D_{n}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
when the test rejection threshold
t=-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)
, and the P-value is estimated by
2\Phi(-|T_{j}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1}|)
. For
D_{n}
to be asymptotically negligible, it suffices that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{n}B_{1}=0
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n})^{2}(\log{n})n^{-\frac{1}{4}}=0
. In Section
3.1
, we have reviewed the related work on the theoretical foundations of the former condition. The latter one is mild if
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}=\log{(n)}
, which is commonly assumed for consistency analysis. Note that the results of Theorem
1
are nonasymptotic. Particularly, the upper bound in (
14
) is uniform over
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
. Therefore, for all large
n
, we may establish a valid VD test for (
2
) for any
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
of interest provided the regularity conditions are satisfied.

Remark 2
.

Model (
1
) is allowed to depend on
n
, but we do not indicate the dependence of our model on
n
explicitly for simplicity.

Let us proceed to the theory for the VDBP test (
10
) with given breaks
a_{1}=\dots=a_{p}=a\in\mathbb{R}
. Recall that
\mu_{l}(a)
for each
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
has been defined in (
7
).

Theorem 2
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
, Condition
3
with
(R,\kappa_{1})=(1,a)
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, and Condition
4
. Assume
\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon|^{8\vee q}<\infty
and Conditions
1
–
2
with
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{\beta_{2}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}+\sqrt{n}B_{1}(-\log B_{1})+n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{q-\beta}+\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\log{(n\vee p)}}=0
for some constants
0<\beta<q
and
0<\beta_{2}<\frac{1}{4}
.
Then
\big[T_{\widehat{l}}(a)-\sqrt{n}\mu_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]^{-1}\overset{D}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1)
. If furthermore
S^{*}=\emptyset
, then
T_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]^{-1}\overset{D}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1)
.

According to Theorem
2
, to have a legitimate VDBP test, feature dimensionality
p
is allowed to grow at some polynomial order of
n
if
\mathbb{E}(|\varepsilon|^{q})<\infty
for sufficiently large
q
. Meanwhile, Theorem
2
assumes

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{n}B_{1}(-\log B_{1})=0
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{\beta_{2}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}=0
. The former condition is only slighly stronger than the analogous condition required by Theorem
1
, while the latter one holds if the former is assumed and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}=\log{n}
. Other comments on regularity conditions of Theorem
2
are similar to those for Theorem
1
, and hence we omit the details here. By Theorem
2
, the asymptotic test size of the VDBP test (
10
) is at most
\alpha
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
when the test rejection threshold
t=-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)
, and the P-value is estimated by
2\Phi(-|T_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]^{-1}|)
.

Next, we analyze the asymptotic properties of the VD (
12
) and VDBP (
13
) tests with break selection in Theorems
3
–
4
below. Here, the sets of
R
break candidates on all coordinates are assumed to be the same, and these break candidates are denoted by
\kappa_{1}<\dots<\kappa_{R}
.

Theorem 3
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{8})<\infty
, and Conditions
1
–
2
and Condition
4
. For
all large
n
, each
0<B_{1}<1
, each
t>0
, and any
1\leq j\leq p
such that Condition
3
holds and
j\not\in S^{*}
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}\big(\big|T_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big]^{-1}\big|\geq t\big)\leq 2\Phi(-t)+D_{n},

where
D_{n}=\varsigma\big\{c\big[\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})\big]^{4}\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{4})\big\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+Rn^{\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}+(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n})^{2}(\log{n})(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+B_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}})
, in which
\varsigma=t(\log{n})(10n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+4B_{1}^{\frac{1}{8}})+n^{\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
c
is given in Condition
3
.

Theorem 4
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
, Condition
3
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, and Condition
4
. In addition, assume
\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon|^{8\vee q}<\infty
and Conditions
1
–
2
with
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{\beta_{2}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}+\sqrt{n}B_{1}(-\log B_{1})+n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{q-\beta}+\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\log{(n\vee p)}}=0
for some constants
0<\beta<q
and
0<\beta_{2}<\frac{1}{4}
.
Then

\big[T_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})-\sqrt{n}\mu_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]^{-1}\overset{D}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).
If furthermore
S^{*}=\emptyset
, then
T_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]^{-1}\overset{D}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

From Theorem
3
, when
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{n}B_{1}=0
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n})^{2}(\log{n})n^{-\frac{1}{4}}=0
are additionally satisfied, it holds that
D_{n}=o(1)
; in this scenario, the test size of (
12
) is at most
\alpha
plus the negligible term
D_{n}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
when the test rejection threshold
t=-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)
, and the P-value is estimated by
2\Phi(-|T_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big]^{-1}|)
. From Theorem
4
, the test size of (
13
) is at most
\alpha
with the rejection threshold
t=-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and the P-value is estimated by
2\Phi(-|T_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]^{-1}|)
. Other comments on these two theorems are similar to those for Theorems
1
–
2
.

4
Test power analysis for VD and VDBP tests

We analyze the test power of the proposed tests respectively in Theorems
5
–
8
in Section
4.2
, with their proofs deferred respectively to the Supplementary Material. The notation of samples, breaks, and break candidates in this section are the same as in Section
3
. We begin with introducing examples of standard deviation functions in model (
1
) with non-empty
S^{*}
.

4.1
Monotonic standard deviation function

Let us introduce below Examples
2
–
4
of standard deviation functions considered in our model (
1
). These examples are commonly used for modeling heteroskedasticity
(
Daye
et al. 2012
)
. Particularly, Example
2
encompasses a wide class of monotonic functions that include the standard deviation functions considered in the aforementioned papers. To simplify the notation, we assume without loss of generality that
S^{*}=\{1,\dots,s^{*}\}
for some integer
s^{*}\geq 1
. Also, for each
j\in S^{*}
, each
\vec{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{s^{*}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}
, and every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
\zeta_{j}(x)
be such that
\zeta_{j}(x)\coloneqq\zeta(z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1},x,z_{j+1},\dots,z_{s^{*}})
if
s^{*}>1
, and
\zeta_{j}(x)\coloneqq\zeta(x)
if
s^{*}=1
. Notice that
\zeta_{j}(x)
is invariant to
z_{j}
.

Example 2
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
, and that for every
j\in S^{*}
and
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}
,
\zeta_{j}(x)
is nondecreasing (or nonincreasing) in
x
. In addition, there exist some
q_{1}<q_{2}
,
\iota>0
, and Cartesian product
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{D}_{s^{*}}
with
\mathcal{D}_{j}\in\mathcal{R}
and
\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{S^{*}}\in\mathcal{D})>0
such that for every
j\in S^{*}
and
\vec{z}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\big|\zeta_{j}(q_{1})-\zeta_{j}(q_{2})\big|>\iota
.

Example 3
.

Let index sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{K}
for some
K>0
be given with
S^{*}=\cup_{k=1}^{K}S_{k}
. Let
\zeta(\vec{z})=\beta_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\Pi_{l\in S_{k}}\bm{1}_{z_{l}\geq b_{kl}}
, in which
\beta_{k},b_{kl}
’s are real values and
\Pi
denotes the product notation.

Example 4
.

\zeta(\vec{z})=\sqrt{\exp\big(\beta_{0}+\sum_{l=1}^{s^{*}}\beta_{l}z_{l}\big)}
with real coefficients
\beta_{0},\dots,\beta_{s^{*}}
.

Example
2
takes into account cases where
\zeta(\cdot)
is a cumulative function. In addition, Examples
3
–
4
with a proper choice of model parameters and some regularity conditions on the distribution of
\bm{X}
are cases of Example
2
. Example
4
is standard in the literature
(
Daye
et al. 2012
;
Smyth and
Verbyla 1999
)
, while Example
3
is motivated by the biological applications mentioned in the Introduction. For example, if the response variable is some biological trait of interest and explanatory features are binary with
X_{j}=1
indicating a mutation at the
j
th genetic position, then
\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})=3+3\times\bm{1}_{\{X_{1}\geq 1\}}\times\bm{1}_{\{X_{2}\geq 1\}}-2\times\bm{1}_{\{X_{3}\geq 1\}}
with
S^{*}=\{1,2,3\}
is an instance of Example
3
that considers interactive effects of features on the conditional variance of the response in model (
1
). We will show in Section
4.2
that when
\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})
satisfies Example
2
with mild additional model assumptions, the consistency of the VD and VDBP tests under their respective alternative hypotheses mainly depends on the variation level
\iota
of the standard deviation function, the sample size, and the feature dimensionality.

4.2
Test power analysis

In this section, we show that
\mu_{l}(\kappa_{r})
for
l\in S^{*}
and
r\in\{1,\dots,R\}
is required to be lower bounded for our tests to be consistent (i.e., the asymptotic test power is one) under the alternative hypotheses, where we recall that
\mu_{l}(\kappa_{r})
has been defined in (
7
). Specifically, for the VD test (
12
) to be consistent when
j\in S^{*}
, we require

\textnormal{Condition }\eqref{split.1}\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{\mu}^{-1}\big(\sqrt{B_{1}}+\sqrt{n}\big)(\log{n})=0,

(15)

in which the signal strength
\underline{\mu}
may decrease as
n
increases, and the dependence on
n
of
\underline{\mu}
is not indicated for simplicity. In addition, the minimum signal strength condition for the VDBP test (
13
) to be consistent when
S^{*}\not=\emptyset
is that

\min_{l\in S^{*}}\max_{1\leq r\leq R}|\mu_{l}(\kappa_{r})|>\underline{\mu}\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{\mu}^{-1}\Big[\sqrt{B_{1}}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{q-\beta}}\sqrt{\log{(n\vee p)}}\Big]=0,

(16)

and
\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon|^{q\vee 4}<\infty
for some
0<\beta<q
, where
\varepsilon
is the model error in model (
1
). From (
15
)–(
16
), the lower bound of
\underline{\mu}
depends on mean estimation error
B_{1}>0
and sample size for the VD test, and it additionally depends on feature dimensionality
p
and moment bound on the model error for the VDBP test. Meanwhile, consistency of the VD (
6
) and VDBP (
10
) tests with given breaks
a_{1}=\dots a_{p}=a\in\mathbb{R}
also relies on similar conditions as (
15
)–(
16
) respectively but with
(R,\kappa_{1})=(1,a)
. Moreover, we give Example
5
below showing that the signal strength requirements (
15
)–(
16
) hold under a general model setting.

Theorems
5
–
6
below analyzes the test power of tests (
6
) and (
10
) with a given break
a\in\mathbb{R}
and that
a_{1}=\dots=a_{p}=a
, respectively; we note that sample variances are assumed to be positive to avoid divisions by zero in this section.

Theorem 5
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{4})<\infty
, and Conditions
1
–
2
such that
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}<\infty
with
B_{1}<1
, and Condition
4
. For
all large
n
, each
t>0
, and any
1\leq j\leq p
such that
|\mu_{j}(a)|>\underline{\mu}>0
, it holds that
\mathbb{P}(|T_{j}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1}|\leq t)\leq B_{1}(\underline{\mu})^{-1}+(\log{n})(1+t)(\sqrt{n}\underline{\mu})^{-1}
.

Theorem 6
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
,
\min_{l\in S^{*}}|\mu_{l}(a)|>\underline{\mu}>0
, Condition
4
, Condition
1
, and
\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon|^{q\vee 4}<\infty
with
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{\mu}^{-1}\Big[\sqrt{B_{1}}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{q-\beta}}\sqrt{\log{(n\vee p)}}\Big]=0
for some
0<\beta<q
. Then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{l}\in S^{*})=1
.
If furthermore Condition
2
holds with
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}<\infty
, then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\big\{\big|T_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(a)\big]^{-1}\big|\leq t\big\}=0
for each
t>0
.

From Theorem
5
that a consistent VD test (
6
) requires
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{\mu}^{-1}\big(B_{1}+\sqrt{n}\big)(\log{n})=0
, which is less restrictive than the requirement in (
15
) because break selection is not needed here. Meanwhile, Theorem
6
states that a valid VDBP test (
10
) needs (
16
), which allows
p
to grow at a polynomial order of
n
given a sufficiently high order moment bound on the model error and that
B_{1}
and
\underline{\mu}
decrease to zero at some proper rate as
n
increases. It is seen that these conditions depends on mean estimation consistency
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}B_{1}=0
, which along with the model boundness condition
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}<\infty
has been commented on in Section
3.2
. More discussion of the signal strength conditions will be given below after Example
5
.

Theorems
7
–
8
below respectively analyze the test power for VD (
12
) and VDBP (
13
) tests with break selection and some predetermined break candidates
\kappa_{1}<\dots<\kappa_{R}
.

Theorem 7
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{4})<\infty
, and Conditions
1
–
2
such that
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}<\infty
with
B_{1}<1
, and Condition
4
. For
all large
n
, each
t>0
, and any
1\leq j\leq p
such that
\max_{1\leq r\leq R}|\mu_{j}(\kappa_{r})|>\underline{\mu}>0
, it holds that
\mathbb{P}(|T_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(\widehat{a}_{j})\big]^{-1}|\leq t)\leq(\log{n})\big[\underline{\mu}^{-1}\sqrt{B_{1}}+(1+t)(\sqrt{n}\underline{\mu})^{-1}\big]
.

Theorem 8
.

Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
,
\min_{l\in S^{*}}\max_{1\leq r\leq R}|\mu_{l}(\kappa_{r})|>\underline{\mu}>0
, Conditions
1
and
4
, and
\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon|^{q\vee 4}<\infty
with
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{\mu}^{-1}\Big[\sqrt{B_{1}}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{q-\beta}}\sqrt{\log{(n\vee p)}}\Big]

=0
for some
0<\beta<q
. Then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{l}\in S^{*})=1
.
If furthermore Condition
2
holds with
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}^{2}B_{1}<\infty
, then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\big|\big[T_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{l}}(\widehat{a}_{\widehat{l}})\big]^{-1}\big|\leq t\big)=0
for each
t>0
.

Theorems
7
–
8
show that the consistency of tests (
12
) and (
13
) with break selection respectively require (
15
) and (
16
), in addition to regularity conditions that have been commented on previously. We now give signal strength lower bounds on
\underline{\mu}
under a general model setting in Example
5
below. Note that Example
2
satisfies the requirements of Example
5
on the standard deviation function.
Without loss of generality, let
S^{*}=\{1,\dots,s^{*}\}
for some integer
s^{*}\geq 1
, and let
\zeta_{j}(x)
be defined as in Section
4.1
.

Example 5
.

Assume that
(\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}})
takes values on
[0,1]^{2p}
and
\underline{c}|A|_{L}<\mathbb{P}\big\{(\bm{X},\widetilde{\bm{X}})\in A\big\}\leq\bar{c}|A|_{L}
for every Borel set
A\subset[0,1]^{2p}
for some
0<\underline{c}\leq\bar{c}
, where
|A|_{L}
is the Lebesgue measure of
A
. Assume
\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})>0
,
\sup_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})<\infty
, and that for every
j\in S^{*}=\{1,\dots,s^{*}\}
and every
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}
,
\zeta_{j}(x)
is nondecreasing (or nonincreasing) in
x
. In addition, for some
0<\delta<\frac{1}{2},\iota>0
, and Cartesian product
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{R}^{s^{*}}
, it holds that
\big|\zeta_{j}(1-\delta)-\zeta_{j}(\delta)\big|>\iota
for every
j\in S^{*}
and every
\vec{z}\in\mathcal{D}
. Then,

\min_{j\in S^{*}}\inf_{\delta<\kappa<1-\delta}|\mu_{j}(\kappa)|>\frac{\iota\delta^{2}\underline{c}}{4\bar{c}}\times\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{S^{*}}\in\mathcal{D})\times\big[\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})\big].

Remark 3
.

Relevance does not imply non-zero signal strength. For example, assume model (
1
) with
\zeta_{1}(\bm{X}_{S_{1}})=\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})
almost surely for some
\zeta_{1}(\cdot)
and
S_{1}\subset\{1,\dots,p\}
. If
j\in S^{*}
and
j\not\in S_{1}
, then
X_{j}
is a relevant feature by definition but
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\mu_{j}(x)|=0
, which is a direct result of Lemma
1
and (
9
) since
\zeta_{1}(\bm{X}_{S_{1}})=\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})
and
j\not\in S_{1}
.

In Example
5
, with
\underline{c},\bar{c},\delta,\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{S^{*}}\in\mathcal{D}),\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})
being positive constants and

\kappa\in[\delta,1-\delta]

(17)

for some
\kappa\in\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{R}\}
, (
15
)–(
16
) are respectively satisfied if

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iota^{-1}\big(\sqrt{B_{1}}+\sqrt{n}\big)(\log{n})=0
and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iota^{-1}\Big[\sqrt{B_{1}}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{q-\beta}}\sqrt{\log{(n\vee p)}}\Big]=0
,
which state that the variation level
\iota
of the standard deviation function cannot be too small in comparison with
\big(\sqrt{B_{1}}+\sqrt{n}\big)(\log{n})
or
\sqrt{B_{1}}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{q-\beta}}\sqrt{\log{(n\vee p)}}
. These terms include the mean estimation error
B_{1}
because model (
1
) has a mean component. On the other hand, (
17
) is satisfied if the range between
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{R}
is reasonably wide and
\kappa_{1}<\dots<\kappa_{R}
are evenly distributed with a proper constant
R>1
.

It is worth noting that the test consistency for tests based on the data-driven break candidates given in Section
2.4
can also be established in ways similar to Theorems
7
–
8
, where break candidates are assumed to be predetermined. Particularly, the set of data-driven break candidates on each coordinate should have a reasonably wide range in a probability sense. However, we omit the detailed analysis for simplicity.

5
Simulation study

We have performed numerous simulation experiments showing that the VD and VDBP tests
compare favorably to existing methods such as DGLM
(
Smyth 1989
)
and high-dimensional extensions of the Breusch-Pagan test
(
Daye
et al. 2012
;
Chiou
et al. 2020
)
in terms of controlling false positive errors. In these experiments, the mean functions may have interaction components with high-dimensional feature inputs. Due to the space limitation, we defer most of our simulation results to the Supplementary Material, and present here a brief comparison of the VDBP test and the standard Breusch-Pagan test.

5.1
Simulation setting and VDBP test for hypothesis (
3
)

We simulate a sample
\mathcal{X}_{n}\coloneqq\{Q_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{Z}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of i.i.d. observations with
n=700
such that
(Q_{1},\bm{Z}_{1})
and
(Y,\bm{X})
have the same distribution given as follows. The response
Y
is generated from one of the following models:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=2X_{1}X_{2}+X_{3}+X_{4}+X_{5}^{2}+\varepsilon,

(18)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=2X_{1}X_{2}+X_{3}+X_{4}+X_{5}^{2}+\Big[1+3\times\bm{1}_{\{X_{15}>0\}}\Big]\varepsilon,

(19)

where
\varepsilon
is an independent standard Gaussian model error, and
\bm{X}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{p})^{\top}
with
p=20
is a multivariate Gaussian vector with zero mean and covariance matrix
\Sigma=[\Sigma_{lk}]_{l,k=1}^{p}
, in which
\Sigma_{lk}=(0.6)^{|l-k|}
. We use the R package
mvtnorm
for sampling
\{\bm{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, while their knockoff features
\{\widetilde{\bm{Z}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are generated by our coordinate-wise Gaussian knockoff generator mentioned in Section
2.1.2
with
\{\bm{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
given.

The VDBP test statistic given by (
13
) is calculated for each simulation experiment, with details given as follows. The simulated sample
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is split into two subsamples
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\{Y_{i},\bm{X}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}=\{V_{i},\bm{U}_{i},\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
with
n_{1}=\lfloor\frac{n}{3}\rceil
and
n_{2}=n-n_{1}
for respectively constructing test statistics and selecting breaks. Our practical implementation of the VDBP test uses the full sample
\mathcal{X}_{n}
for training the random forests estimate of mean functions.
In addition, for each
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, we set break candidates
(\kappa_{1,l},\kappa_{R,l})
to respectively the first and third quartiles of
(U_{1l},\dots,U_{n_{2}l}
) with
R=100
,
\kappa_{r,l}<\kappa_{r+1,l}
, and evenly distributed
\kappa_{r,l}
’s. By Theorem
4
, we set the rejection thresholds
(t_{0.1},t_{0.05})=(1.64,1.96)
for the VDBP test. Meanwhile, the benchmark method here is the Breusch-Pagan test
(
Breusch and
Pagan 1979
)
, whose P-value is available from the R package
lmtest
. We report the empirical rejection rates of each case over
100
repetitions in Table
1
, with details indicated in each panel.

5.2
Simulation results

In Table
1
, the VDBP test compares favorably to the Breusch-Pagan test in terms of controlling the empirical wrong rejection rates under model (
18
), while the test power of the Breusch-Pagan test is slightly better than the VDBP test under model (
19
). It is seen that the Breusch-Pagan test does not control the false positive error rates at the target significant levels. Such results are expected because the mean functions here contain some nonlinear components, which cannot be dealt with by most existing tests for heteroskedasticity. These results are the basic motivation for the proposed tests. For more simulation results, see the Supplementary Material.

Table 2:
Each explanatory feature in the HIV-1 dataset is a pair of position/mutation type. Sizes of available samples differ across drugs since each participant is usually treated with a few drugs. Mutation positions of those pairs in curly brackets are in the ground truth sets of active features for the (conditional) means of drug resistance levels.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 1:
The drug names and mutations of interest (genetic position, mutation type) are indicated on the top of each picture. In each picture, samples on the left-hand side of the dashed line indicate those samples having the mutation of interest; for example, in panel SQV, samples on the left of the dashed line have mutation (46, I).

In this section, we apply the VD test to study which human genetic mutations are associated with the conditional variance of drug resistance level for Human Immunodeficiency Virus Type 1 (HIV-1)
(
Rhee et al. 2006
)
. Our inferences provides further understanding of which mutations contribute to the risk of having high drug resistance. Such results complement the previous works on the inference for identifying mutations that are associated with the conditional mean of drug resistance level
(
Syrgkanis and
Zampetakis 2020
;
Barber and
Candès 2019
)
.

In this HIV-1 dataset,
16
drugs for treating HIV-1 are considered: seven protease
inhibitors (PIs), six nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NRTIs), and three
nonnucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NNRTIs). Each participant with HIV-1 is treated with some of these drugs. For each drug, the resulting drug resistance levels and these participants’ genetic information are observed and recorded, with the former taken as the response variable and the latter taken as the explanatory features; each explanatory feature is binary and indicates whether the individual has the genetic mutation at a specific genetic position or not. Accordingly, we have
16
samples for each drug as displayed in Table
2
. For each of these samples, the resistance levels
are log-transformed
(
Barber and
Candès 2019
)
; meanwhile, we remove duplicate features, features with zero mutations, and observations with missing values from each sample. The number of binary features and sample size of each sample are reported in Table
2
. In addition, the distribution of mutations are assumed to follow a hidden Markov model (HMM) distribution
(
Serfling 2009
)
, and their knockoff features are generated using the HMM knockoffs developed by Sesia et al.
(
Serfling 2009
)
with default setting. See Section
D.2
of the Supplementary Material for a numerical experiment assuring the reliability of HMM knockoffs for our HIV-1 study. With knockoff features, the VD statistic in (
6
) is calculated for each feature with the full sample for training the random forests centering model, and a fixed break at
0.5
. Their P-values are obtained according to Theorem
1
.

In Table
2
, we report the significant mutations at
\alpha=0.01
or FDR
=0.2
, where each mutation is denoted by a pair (mutation position, type), and the FDR is controlled by the method Q-value
(
Sorensen and
Waagepetersen 2003
)
in the R package
qvalue
. In this table, pairs in curly brackets are those whose mutation positions appear in known sets of active mutations for conditional means of drug resistance levels. These sets of mutations are identified by another experiment and are taken as the “ground truth” sets of active mutations for conditional means. Details of these mutations are given in the mutation set “TSMs” in Table 1 of
(
Rhee et al. 2006
)
.

We also provide preliminary diagnosis in Figure
1
showing the effects of significant mutations on the conditional variance of drug resistance level. These pictures respectively present residual plots for the SQV, EFV, and NFV samples, where residuals are given by
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})
’s, in which
Y_{i}
’s are drug resistance levels,
\bm{X}_{i}
’s are binary vectors of mutations for each individual, and
\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})
’s are the random forests predictions for
Y_{i}
’s. The x-axis in these figures are observation indices, and observed data on the left-hand side of the dashed lines are those having the significant mutations of interest, which are indicated on the top of each plot. Other details are in their figure captions.

6.2
Results

Our study of HIV-1 drug resistance in Table
2
and Figure
1
reveals some interesting results, which are reviewed as follows. From Table
2
, mutations (63, P) and (74, V) are significant while FDR is controlled at
0.2
, but they are not in the ground truth sets for mean effects on drug resistance. These results suggest that there may exist more general distributional relations between mutations and drug resistance than mean effects that have been studied by
(
Rhee et al. 2006
;
Barber and
Candès 2019
)
. In addition, Figure
1
provides an informative preliminary diagnosis for the log-transformed drug resistance levels. There, we see that some significant mutations have clear effects on conditional variance (e.g. the panels SQV and EFV) and some have less clear effects on conditional variance (e.g. the panel NFV). We note that in general, mutations with clear effects on conditional variance in the preliminary diagnosis could be spurious mutations; for example, some irrelevant mutations may be correlated with relevant ones. It is therefore important to apply an appropriate test such as the VD test to assess significance. On the whole, our findings should encourage further investigation into the effects of mutations on variability of HIV-1 drug resistance, which may help develop more effective HIV-1 drugs with reduced drug resistance variability and optimize the use of available drugs.

7
Discussions

In this paper, we have established rigorous P-values and test sizes for the VD and VDBP tests, and we have also analyzed the test power under a nonparametric heteroskedastic data generating model with high-dimensional input features, which, to our knowledge, are the first rigorous tests for high-dimensional regression heteroskedasticity that allow nonlinear mean regression and flexible standard deviation regression. There are several potential extensions of our work. For example, it may be possible to strengthen our tests so they are applicable to heteroskedastic models with non-monotonic standard deviation functions. Such an extension takes into account squared components in
\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})
, which are common heteroskedastic components in economics and finance. In addition, it is possible to incorporate the idea of coordinate-wise knockoff into existing knockoff generators
(
Romano
et al. 2020
;
Jordon
et al. 2018
;
Lu
et al. 2018
)
. With these coordinate-wise distribution-free knockoff generators, we may apply the VD and VDBP tests to cases with unknown feature distribution. These applications are also interesting future work.

, is available upon request.

Supplementary Material to “Testing for Regression Heteroskedasticity with High-Dimensional Random Forests”

Chien-Ming Chi

This Supplementary Material contains the proofs of Theorems
1
–
8
in Section
A
, the proofs of Lemma
1
and Example
5
in Section

, and some technical lemmas and their proofs in Section

. Simulation experiments for the VD and VDBP tests with synthetic data are in Section
D.1
, and the numerical experiment for hidden Markov model knockoffs
(
Serfling 2009
)
are in Section

. In addition, in Section
E
, we develop a coordinate-wise Gaussian knockoff generator for our simulation experiments; see Section
D.1.1
and Section
E
for details.

All the notation is the same as defined in the main body of the paper. In addition, we use the following notation in the Supplementary Material. For a matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
of size
(K\times L)
, we use
\Sigma_{kl}
to denote its entry at the
k
th row and
l
th column. Throughout the proofs, we use generic constants such as
C
and
K
; unless specified otherwise, these constants are independent of the sample size. For convenience, we use the little
o
and big
O
notation: for two real sequences
\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
,
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
means that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}=0
;
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
means that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{|a_{n}|}{|b_{n}|}<\infty
.

Appendix A
Proof of theorems

A.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Let us begin with a remark that model (
1
) is allowed to depend on sample size
n
, and we do not indicate the dependence of model (
1
) on
n
explicitly for simplicity. In addition, recall that we have defined
\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})=\zeta_{0}
if
S^{*}=\emptyset
in Section
1.1
; the proof here applies to cases with empty or non-empty
S^{*}
.

Now, the formal proof of Theorem
1
begins with (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) below. We will briefly explain our proof strategy after the inequality (
A.2
).

\begin{split}T_{j}(a)&=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}\\
&=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)(m(\bm{X}_{i})-\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i}))^{2}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt-2n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)(m(\bm{X}_{i})-\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i}))\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)(\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i})^{2}\\
&\eqqcolon A_{1}+A_{2}+T_{j}^{(\star)}(a),\end{split}

(A.1)

where
\mathcal{A}=(-\infty,a]
,
T_{j}^{(\star)}(a)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big[\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{2}
, and
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})
. In addition,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(|T_{j}(a)\big[\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)\big]^{-1}|\geq t)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}(|T_{j}^{(\star)}(a)|\geq t\big[\sigma_{j}(a)\big]-\varsigma-t|\sigma_{j}(a)-\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)|-|A_{1}|-|A_{2}|+\varsigma),\end{split}

(A.2)

where

\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)\coloneqq\textnormal{Var}\big\{\big[\bm{1}_{X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{2}\big\}=\mathbb{E}\big\{\big[\bm{1}_{X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]^{2}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{4}\big\}

since

\mathbb{E}\big\{\big[\bm{1}_{X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{2}\big\}=0,

(A.3)

which is due to the assumption that
j\not\in S^{*}
and the definition of knockoff features (see (
9
) for details), and we assume
\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)>0
to avoid division by zero for simplicity.

From (
A.2
), it is seen that the desired result follows if
\varsigma\geq t|\sigma_{j}(a)-\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)|+|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|
in a probability sense and that
\mathbb{P}(|T_{j}^{(\star)}(a)|\geq t\big[\sigma_{j}(a)\big]-\varsigma)
is bounded by
2\Phi(-t)
plus an additional term, which is negligible if
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{n}B_{1}+(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n})^{2}(\log{n})n^{-\frac{1}{4}}=0
(see the comments after Theorem
1
in the main text for details). In the following, we will show that these two results both hold. Recall that
\varsigma=t(\log{n})(10n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+4B_{1}^{\frac{1}{8}})+n^{\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

First, we establish the upper bounds for
|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)-\sigma_{j}(a)|
,
|A_{1}|
, and
|A_{2}|
, and begin with the one for
|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)-\sigma_{j}(a)|
. By Condition
3
with
(R,\kappa_{1})=(1,a)
and that
\varepsilon
is independent of
(\bm{X},\widetilde{X}_{j})
, it holds that

\begin{split}\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)&=\mathbb{E}\big\{\big[\bm{1}_{X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]^{2}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{4}\big\}\\
&\geq\mathbb{E}\big\{\bm{1}_{\{X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}\}\cap\{\widetilde{X}_{j}\in\mathcal{A}^{c}\}}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{S^{*}})\varepsilon\big]^{4}\big\}\\
&\geq c\big[\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})\big]^{4}\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{4}),\end{split}

(A.4)

where
c>0
is defined in Condition
3
. The result of (
A.4
) leads to

\begin{split}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)-\sigma_{j}(a)|&=\frac{|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}(a)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)|}{\widehat{\sigma}_{j}(a)+\sigma_{j}(a)}\\
&\leq\frac{|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}(a)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)|}{\sigma_{j}(a)}\\
&\leq\big\{c\big[\inf_{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{*}}}\zeta(\vec{z})\big]^{4}\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{4})\big\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}(a)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)|.\end{split}

(A.5)

Next, we write

\begin{split}&\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}(a)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)\\
&=-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)-\big[\widehat{\mu}_{j}(a)\big]^{2}+n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big[\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i})^{4}\\
&=-\big[\widehat{\mu}_{j}(a)\big]^{2}+\bigg\{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\{\big[\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]^{2}\times\Big\{\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]^{4}\\
&\qquad\qquad-4\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]^{3}\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}+6\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]^{2}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad-4\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{3}\Big\}\Big\}\bigg\}\\
&\qquad+n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\{\big[\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]^{2}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{4}-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)\Big\},\end{split}

(A.6)

where
\widehat{\mu}_{j}(a)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)\big(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}\big)^{2}
, and define events
E_{1},\dots,E_{8}
as follows.

\begin{split}E_{1}&=\left\{\left|n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)\big[m(\bm{X}_{i})-\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})\big]^{2}\right|\leq n^{\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},\\
E_{2}&=\left\{\left|2n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)\big[m(\bm{X}_{i})-\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})\big]\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\right|\leq B_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}\right\},\\
E_{3}&=\left\{\left|n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big)\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{2}\right|\leq n^{\frac{1}{4}}\right\},\\
E_{4}&=\left\{\left|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}})^{2}\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]^{4}\right|\leq B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},\\
E_{5}&=\left\{\left|4n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}})^{2}\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]^{3}\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\right|\leq B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},\\
E_{6}&=\left\{\left|4n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}})^{2}\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{3}\right|\leq B_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}\right\},\\
E_{7}&=\left\{\left|6n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}})^{2}\big[\widehat{m}(\bm{X}_{i})-m(\bm{X}_{i})\big]^{2}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{2}\right|\leq B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},\\
E_{8}&=\left\{\left|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\big[\bm{1}_{X_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}-\bm{1}_{\widetilde{X}_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}}\big]^{2}\big[\zeta(\bm{X}_{iS^{*}})\varepsilon_{i}\big]^{4}-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)\big\}\right|\leq n^{-\frac{1}{4}}\right\}.\end{split}

In light of (
A.5
) and these events, we now establish the upper bounds for
|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}(a)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(a)|
,
|A_{1}|
, and
|A_{2}|
. On
\cap_{l=1}^{3}E_{l}
,

|\widehat{\mu}_{j}(a)|\leq n^{-\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}+n^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(A.7)

On
\cap_{l=1}^{2}E_{l}
,

|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|\leq n^{\frac{1}{4}}B_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}+B_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}.

By (
A.6
)–(
A.7
), on
\cap_{l=1}^{8}E_{l}
, for each
